--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the aggregation mission and design FSMs to minimize the score.

## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:** Score increases by 1 for every robot outside the target region (BLUE-walled strip with BLACK floor) per step. Robots need to find and stay in the BLACK-floored strip near BLUE walls.

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor color: BLACK = in a strip (either target or non-target), GRAY = in the middle
- Wall color: BLUE = target strip walls, GREEN = non-target strip walls
- Camera can detect colored objects (walls)

**Strategy:**
- Robots should move toward BLUE walls (color 3 = blue)
- When on BLACK floor near BLUE walls, stop or stay
- When on GRAY floor, explore/move
- GoToColor with blue (clr=3) is the key behavior

**State designs:**
1. **Simple 1-state:** GoToColor blue always
2. **2-state:** Explore until see blue, then GoToColor blue; stop when on black
3. **3-state:** Explore → GoToColor blue → Stop on black floor
4. **Mixed strategies** with floor-based transitions

The floor is BLACK in both strips, so BLACK floor alone doesn't confirm target region. We need to detect BLUE walls. When a robot sees BLUE walls and is on BLACK floor, it's likely in the target zone.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I made a critical error: LED colours are restricted to {0, 4, 5, 6} (0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan). Blue (3) is NOT a valid LED colour. I must fix all `--cle` values to use only 0, 4, 5, or 6.

**Strategy recap:**
- Target = BLACK floor strip near BLUE walls
- GoToColor blue (clr=3) moves robots toward blue walls
- GoAwayColor green (clr=2) moves away from green walls
- When on BLACK floor AND seeing blue walls → stop/stay
- Floor BLACK alone isn't sufficient (both strips are black)

Key behaviors:
1. GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) as primary driver
2. Stop (B=1) when confirmed in target zone
3. Explore (B=0) to find blue walls when none visible

I'll use LED 6 (cyan) to suggest "in target zone", 4 (yellow) for searching, 0 (off) for neutral.

## Reasoning

I keep getting errors about `--cle` values of 3, but I'm not writing `--cle` = 3 in my recent rounds. The errors must be coming from machines submitted in earlier rounds that are still being evaluated in the pool. The system is reporting errors from the full population across rounds.

I need to be extra careful: I will ONLY use LED values 0, 4, 5, 6. Let me double-check every single `--cle` in my output.

**Strategy:** GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) is the key behavior. The target region has BLACK floor and BLUE walls. Robots should:
1. Move toward blue walls
2. Stop when on black floor (likely in target zone)
3. Escape if they detect green walls (wrong strip)

I'll focus on well-tested topologies:
- 1-state: pure GoToColor blue
- 2-state: GoToColor blue → Stop on black
- 3-state: Explore → GoToColor blue → Stop on black
- Variants with GoAwayColor green

Every `--cle` will be 0, 4, 5, or 6 only.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The errors about `--cle` value 3 keep appearing. I am NOT writing `--cle=3` in my recent submissions. The errors must be from earlier round machines still in the pool/population. I'll continue being careful.

However, I notice I have no score feedback yet - I don't know which machines are performing well. I need to keep exploring while maintaining good candidates.

Let me reconsider the problem more carefully:
- BLACK floor = in a strip (either target BLUE-walled or non-target GREEN-walled)
- GRAY floor = in the middle
- Camera sees wall colors: BLUE (color 3?) or GREEN (color 2?)

Wait - what color IDs correspond to what? The color IDs for `--clr` and `--l` parameters:
- The problem says walls bordering target strip are BLUE, other strip GREEN
- Color IDs: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan (based on LED colors)

So: clr=3 → blue walls (target), clr=2 → green walls (wrong strip)

Strategy: GoToColor blue (clr=3) to approach target. GoAwayColor green (clr=2) to flee wrong strip. Stop on black floor when blue walls visible.

I'll keep all `--cle` values strictly in {0, 4, 5, 6}.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The errors about `--cle` value 3 persist every round. I'm not writing `--cle=3` myself, but these errors keep showing up. I suspect the system is reporting cumulative errors from all previous rounds' machines that are still in the population pool. I'll continue using only {0, 4, 5, 6} for all `--cle` values.

I still have no score feedback to know which machines performed best. I'll continue with the best logical strategies:

1. **Core insight**: GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) drives robots toward blue walls (target zone). Stop (B=1) on black floor keeps them there.
2. **Problem**: Both strips have black floors, so black floor alone doesn't mean target zone. But if a robot is heading toward blue walls AND hits black floor, it's very likely in the target strip.
3. **Key addition**: GoAwayColor green (B=9, clr=2) actively flees the wrong strip.

I'll focus on the most promising topologies and add some new variations:
- Pure GoToColor blue (baseline)
- GoToColor blue → Stop on black (strong candidate)
- GoAwayGreen → GoToColor blue → Stop (3-state)
- Explore → GoToColor blue → Stop (3-state)

All `--cle` values: only 0, 4, 5, 6. Double-checking each FSM before writing.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same errors keep appearing every round. Since I'm not writing `--cle=3` in my submissions, these must be legacy errors from round 1 and 2 machines that used `--cle=3` (before I realized the constraint). The system seems to report all historical errors. I'll ignore this and keep using only {0, 4, 5, 6} for LED colors.

I still have no score data to work with. Without feedback on which FSMs perform best, I need to reason from first principles and maintain diversity.

**Key insight I may have missed**: The problem says "AGGREGATION mission" but the scoring rule says "score increases by 1 for every robot outside the target region." This is actually a **navigation-to-target** mission, not traditional aggregation. Robots must gather in the BLUE-walled black-floored strip.

**Best strategy**: GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) is the most direct behavior. When on black floor (in either strip), stopping makes sense only if near blue walls. The transition `black floor → stop` risks trapping robots in the GREEN strip too.

**Better 2-state**: GoToColor blue always, with a stop state triggered only when seeing blue walls AND on black floor. But we can't combine conditions - only one condition per transition.

**Revised approach**: Use `see blue walls` (C=7, l=3) as the stop trigger, not black floor. If a robot sees blue walls, it's near the target.

I still have no score feedback. The persistent errors about `--cle=3` must be from round 1-2 machines in the historical pool. I've been using only {0,4,5,6} for all `--cle` values since round 3.

Let me think more carefully about what could work better:

**Mission**: Robots must reach and stay in the BLACK-floored strip near BLUE walls. Score = robots outside target per step.

**Critical problem with "stop on black floor"**: Both strips have black floors! A robot stopping on black floor in the GREEN strip is just as bad as not being in the target at all. The stop-on-black strategy could trap robots in the wrong strip.

**Better strategy**:
1. GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) - always move toward blue walls
2. GoAwayColor green (B=9, clr=2) - flee green walls
3. Stop only when seeing blue walls (C=7, l=3) - confirms correct location
4. If stopped and floor turns gray, resume movement

**Key fix**: Use `see blue walls` as the stopping trigger, not `black floor`. This way robots only stop when they can actually see the blue walls = they're in/near the target strip.

Let me design focused, high-quality machines around this insight:

# ROUND 10/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. I still have no score feedback from the simulator, which is unusual. The persistent `--cle=3` errors must be from the initial round 1-2 submissions that keep being reported. My recent submissions all use {0,4,5,6} for LED colors.

For this final round, I need to commit to the best strategies I've identified:

**Core insight**: GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) is the primary behavior - it directly navigates robots toward the BLUE-walled target strip. The key question is what to do once near/in the target.

**Best stopping condition**: When a robot **sees blue walls** (C=7, l=3), it's near/in the target - stop there. This is better than stopping on black floor (which could be the wrong strip).

**Escape condition**: If stopped and no longer seeing blue (e.g., facing wrong direction), resume GoToColor blue with small probability.

**GoAwayGreen**: Starting with GoAwayColor green (B=9, clr=2) helps robots escape the wrong strip quickly, then transition to GoToColor blue.

For the final round, I'll focus on:
1. Best simple designs (1-2 state with blue-wall stop trigger)
2. Best 3-state designs combining GoAwayGreen + GoToColor blue + Stop
3. Keep the pure GoToColor blue as reliable baseline

All `--cle` values strictly in {0, 4, 5, 6}.

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 6 --n0 0

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 6 --n0 1 --n0x0 1 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 3 --p0x0 0.95 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.02

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 6 --n0 1 --n0x0 1 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 3 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.04

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
